<a id='question-098'></a>

### Question 098: Liver Pathology: Focus 02-48 (is by serology.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology of Wilson disease, specifically the molecular defect leading to copper accumulation, and its histological manifestations in the liver. (本题考察威尔逊病的病理生理学，特别是导致铜积累的分子缺陷及其在肝脏中的组织学表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents to the clinic with progressive fatigue, jaundice, and abdominal swelling over the past 6 months. He has a history of psychiatric symptoms, including personality changes and tremors, which began 2 years ago. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he denies alcohol use or illicit drug use. Family history reveals that his older sister died at age 35 from "liver failure." On physical examination, he exhibits Kayser-Fleischer rings (golden-brown rings around the cornea), hepatosplenomegaly, and signs of chronic liver disease (ascites, spider angiomata). Laboratory tests show elevated serum aminotransferases (AST 180 U/L, ALT 150 U/L), elevated bilirubin (total 4.5 mg/dL), low serum ceruloplasmin (5 mg/dL; normal range 20-60 mg/dL), and increased 24-hour urinary copper excretion (1500 mcg; normal range <100 mcg). A liver biopsy is performed. Histopathology reveals diffuse hepatocellular necrosis, steatosis, and inflammation, with prominent copper deposition demonstrated by rhodanine stain.

Which of the following molecular defects is the underlying cause of this patient's liver disease?

(A) Deficiency of alpha-1 antitrypsin leading to misfolded protein accumulation in hepatocytes.
(B) Mutation in the *ATP7B* gene impairing copper transport into bile.
(C) Defective synthesis of apolipoprotein B-100 causing impaired secretion of very-low-density lipoproteins (VLDL).
(D) Accumulation of iron within hepatocytes due to impaired hepcidin regulation.
(E) Defective lysosomal enzyme activity leading to glycogen accumulation in hepatocytes.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 28-year-old male with progressive liver disease symptoms (fatigue, jaundice, ascites), psychiatric symptoms (personality changes, tremors), and Kayser-Fleischer rings. Family history of early death from liver failure in a sibling.
- **Physical Exam:** Kayser-Fleischer rings (pathognomonic for copper deposition), hepatosplenomegaly, signs of chronic liver disease.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated liver enzymes (AST, ALT), elevated bilirubin (jaundice), *low serum ceruloplasmin* (key finding), *increased urinary copper excretion* (key finding).
- **Liver Biopsy:** Hepatocellular necrosis, steatosis, inflammation, *copper deposition* (confirmed by rhodanine stain).
- **Synthesis:** The constellation of clinical features (liver disease, neurological/psychiatric symptoms, Kayser-Fleischer rings), family history, and specific lab findings (low ceruloplasmin, high urinary copper, copper deposition in liver) strongly points towards Wilson disease. The question asks for the *molecular defect* underlying this condition.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Wilson Disease Pathophysiology:** Wilson disease is an autosomal recessive disorder of copper metabolism. The primary defect lies in the *ATP7B* gene, located on chromosome 13. This gene encodes a copper-transporting P-type ATPase protein, primarily expressed in the liver.
- **ATP7B Function:** The ATP7B protein has two main functions:
    1.  Incorporating copper into apoceruloplasmin to form ceruloplasmin (the major copper-carrying protein in the blood).
    2.  Transporting excess copper from the hepatocyte into the bile for excretion.
- **Molecular Defect:** Mutations in *ATP7B* lead to a dysfunctional or absent protein. This results in:
    1.  Impaired incorporation of copper into apoceruloplasmin, leading to low serum ceruloplasmin levels.
    2.  Impaired biliary excretion of copper, causing copper to accumulate within hepatocytes.
- **Consequences of Copper Accumulation:** Excess copper is toxic to cells, particularly hepatocytes and neurons. In the liver, it causes oxidative damage, leading to inflammation, necrosis, fibrosis, and eventually cirrhosis. In the brain, it accumulates in the basal ganglia, causing neurological and psychiatric symptoms. Copper also deposits in the cornea, forming Kayser-Fleischer rings.
- **Connecting to the Vignette:** The patient's low ceruloplasmin, high urinary copper, liver biopsy findings (copper deposition), and clinical presentation (liver disease, neurological symptoms, Kayser-Fleischer rings) are all consistent with the consequences of a defective ATP7B protein. Therefore, the underlying molecular defect is a mutation in the *ATP7B* gene impairing copper transport, specifically into bile.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Deficiency of alpha-1 antitrypsin:** This leads to alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency, characterized by accumulation of misfolded alpha-1 antitrypsin protein within hepatocytes, causing liver damage (cirrhosis) and increased susceptibility to emphysema. While it causes liver disease, it does not typically present with low ceruloplasmin, high urinary copper, Kayser-Fleischer rings, or neurological symptoms like tremors. The molecular defect is in the *SERPINA1* gene, not related to copper transport.
- (C): **Defective synthesis of apolipoprotein B-100:** This is characteristic of abetalipoproteinemia (Bassen-Kornzweig syndrome), an autosomal recessive disorder affecting VLDL and chylomicron assembly and secretion. It leads to fat malabsorption, vitamin deficiencies (A, D, E, K), neurological problems (ataxia, retinitis pigmentosa), and acanthocytosis. It does not cause copper accumulation or the specific findings seen in Wilson disease.
- (D): **Accumulation of iron within hepatocytes:** This describes hemochromatosis, typically caused by mutations in the *HFE* gene (most common) or other genes involved in iron regulation. It leads to iron overload in various organs, including the liver (cirrhosis), heart (cardiomyopathy), pancreas (diabetes), and skin (bronze discoloration). It does not involve copper metabolism defects, low ceruloplasmin, or Kayser-Fleischer rings.
- (E): **Defective lysosomal enzyme activity leading to glycogen accumulation:** This describes glycogen storage diseases (GSDs). Several types affect the liver (e.g., GSD type I, III, IV, VI, IX). They lead to hepatomegaly and liver dysfunction due to glycogen accumulation. They are unrelated to copper metabolism and do not cause the specific clinical and laboratory findings of Wilson disease.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Wilson Disease (ATP7B mutation) | Alpha-1 Antitrypsin Deficiency | Abetalipoproteinemia (ApoB-100 defect) | Hemochromatosis (Iron overload) | Glycogen Storage Disease |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------ | :----------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :----------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Copper transport (ATP7B)        | A1AT protein folding (SERPINA1) | ApoB-100 synthesis/secretion        | Iron regulation (e.g., HFE)    | Lysosomal enzyme defect |
| **Accumulating Substance** | Copper                          | Misfolded A1AT protein         | Lipids (in enterocytes)              | Iron                           | Glycogen                 |
| **Liver Pathology**     | Necrosis, steatosis, fibrosis, cirrhosis, copper deposition | PAS+ globules, cirrhosis             | Steatosis                       | Fibrosis, cirrhosis, hemosiderin | Glycogen accumulation    |
| **Serum Ceruloplasmin** | Low                             | Normal                         | Normal                               | Normal                         | Normal                   |
| **Urinary Copper**      | High                            | Normal                         | Normal                               | Normal                         | Normal                   |
| **Kayser-Fleischer Rings** | Present                         | Absent                        | Absent                               | Absent                        | Absent                   |
| **Neurological Sx**    | Common (tremors, personality change) | Rare                           | Common (ataxia, retinitis pigmentosa) | Rare                           | Rare                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wilson disease is caused by mutations in the *ATP7B* gene, leading to impaired copper excretion into bile and incorporation into ceruloplasmin. Key diagnostic features include low serum ceruloplasmin, high urinary copper, Kayser-Fleischer rings, and liver biopsy showing copper deposition. Differentiate it from other causes of liver disease like alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency (PAS+ globules), hemochromatosis (iron overload), and glycogen storage diseases (glycogen accumulation).

<a id='question-099'></a>

### Question 099: Liver Pathology: Focus 02-49 (Acute viral hepatitis is viral hepatitis with sign)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the histopathological features of acute viral hepatitis, specifically the characteristic inflammatory infiltrate and hepatocellular injury patterns, and differentiating it from other causes of liver injury. (理解病毒性肝炎的组织病理学特征，特别是其特征性的炎症浸润和肝细胞损伤模式，并将其与其他肝损伤原因区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male intravenous drug user presents to the emergency department with a 3-week history of fatigue, anorexia, nausea, and jaundice. He denies alcohol use. His past medical history is significant for Hepatitis C virus (HCV) infection diagnosed 5 years ago, for which he is currently undergoing treatment with sofosbuvir and ledipasvir. On physical examination, he is jaundiced, with scleral icterus and mild right upper quadrant tenderness. Vital signs are stable. Laboratory tests reveal elevated liver enzymes: AST 1200 U/L (normal <40 U/L), ALT 1500 U/L (normal <40 U/L), alkaline phosphatase 250 U/L (normal <120 U/L), and total bilirubin 5 mg/dL (normal <1.2 mg/dL). Hepatitis A IgM antibody is positive, while Hepatitis B surface antigen and Hepatitis C viral load are negative. A liver biopsy is performed. Histopathology shows diffuse hepatocellular necrosis, predominantly in the midzonal (zone 2) and periportal (zone 1) regions, accompanied by a prominent inflammatory infiltrate composed mainly of lymphocytes and plasma cells, with scattered apoptotic bodies and cholestasis within canaliculi. Kupffer cells are hypertrophied.

Which of the following histological findings is MOST characteristic of this patient's liver biopsy?

(A) Councilman bodies and Mallory-Denk bodies within hepatocytes.
(B) Bridging fibrosis and pseudoacinar formation.
(C) Acidophil bodies and portal lymphoid aggregates with bile duct injury.
(D) Steatosis and ballooning degeneration of hepatocytes.
(E) Cholestatic granulomas and vanishing bile duct syndrome.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male IV drug user, history of HCV (currently treated), presenting with acute symptoms (fatigue, anorexia, nausea, jaundice) for 3 weeks.
- **Key Findings:** Jaundice, elevated AST/ALT (significantly >1000 U/L), elevated bilirubin, positive Hepatitis A IgM, negative HBsAg and HCV viral load. This points strongly towards acute Hepatitis A infection superimposed on his treated HCV.
- **Liver Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse hepatocellular necrosis (midzonal/periportal), prominent lymphocytic/plasma cell infiltrate, apoptotic bodies, cholestasis, hypertrophied Kupffer cells.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST characteristic histological finding.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Acute Viral Hepatitis (HAV):** HAV is an RNA virus that replicates primarily in hepatocytes. The immune response, particularly cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs), targets infected hepatocytes, leading to hepatocellular injury and necrosis.
- **Histopathology of Acute Viral Hepatitis:** The characteristic features include:
    - **Hepatocellular Necrosis:** Often midzonal (zone 2) or periportal (zone 1), reflecting the distribution of viral replication and immune attack.
    - **Inflammation:** Primarily lymphocytic and plasma cell infiltrate, often prominent in portal tracts (portal lymphoid aggregates) and extending into the lobule.
    - **Apoptotic Bodies:** Scattered throughout the liver parenchyma, representing individual dying hepatocytes.
    - **Cholestasis:** Bile plugs within canaliculi due to impaired bile flow secondary to hepatocellular injury and inflammation.
    - **Kupffer Cell Hypertrophy:** Reactive hyperplasia of Kupffer cells (liver macrophages).
    - **Acidophil Bodies:** These are shrunken, intensely eosinophilic (pink) hepatocytes resulting from severe hepatocellular injury and necrosis, often seen in acute viral hepatitis. They are essentially apoptotic/necrotic hepatocytes undergoing rapid degradation.
- **Differential Diagnosis based on Histology:**
    - **Alcoholic Hepatitis:** Mallory-Denk bodies (hyaline inclusions), neutrophilic infiltrate, ballooning degeneration.
    - **Chronic Hepatitis:** Bridging fibrosis, pseudoacinar formation (interface hepatitis), plasma cell infiltrate.
    - **Non-alcoholic Steatohepatitis (NASH):** Steatosis, ballooning degeneration, Mallory-Denk bodies (less common than in alcoholic hepatitis), inflammation.
    - **Cholestatic Liver Diseases (e.g., PBC, PSC):** Cholestasis, bile duct injury/proliferation, granulomas (PBC), fibrosis.
    - **Drug-Induced Liver Injury (DILI):** Variable patterns, can mimic acute hepatitis, cholestasis, or steatohepatitis.

- **Connecting Vignette to Histology:** The patient's clinical presentation (acute onset, jaundice, high transaminases, positive HAV IgM) and biopsy findings (diffuse necrosis, lymphocytic infiltrate, apoptotic bodies, cholestasis) are classic for acute viral hepatitis, specifically Hepatitis A in this case. Acidophil bodies are a hallmark histological feature of acute hepatocellular injury, particularly prominent in acute viral hepatitis. Portal lymphoid aggregates are also characteristic of the immune response in acute viral hepatitis. Bile duct injury is less common but can occur.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Councilman bodies** are apoptotic hepatocytes, which *are* seen in acute viral hepatitis (often called acidophil bodies in this context). However, **Mallory-Denk bodies** are characteristic of alcoholic hepatitis or NASH, not typically acute viral hepatitis. This option combines features of both, making it incorrect.
- (B): **Bridging fibrosis** and **pseudoacinar formation** (interface hepatitis) are characteristic features of *chronic* hepatitis, not acute viral hepatitis. Acute hepatitis typically lacks significant fibrosis.
- (C): **Acidophil bodies** (apoptotic/necrotic hepatocytes) and **portal lymphoid aggregates** (lymphocytic infiltrate in portal tracts) are indeed characteristic features of acute viral hepatitis. Bile duct injury can sometimes be seen but is less prominent than in cholestatic diseases. This option accurately describes key findings.
- (D): **Steatosis** (fatty change) and **ballooning degeneration** are characteristic features of NASH or alcoholic hepatitis, not typically the primary findings in acute viral hepatitis, although some steatosis can occur.
- (E): **Cholestatic granulomas** are characteristic of Primary Biliary Cholangitis (PBC). **Vanishing bile duct syndrome** is seen in conditions like chronic graft-versus-host disease or certain drug reactions, not acute viral hepatitis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Acute Viral Hepatitis (HAV) | Alcoholic Hepatitis | NASH                 | Chronic Hepatitis | PBC                     |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------- | :------------------ | :------------------- | :---------------- | :---------------------- |
| **Hepatocellular Injury** | Diffuse necrosis (midzonal/periportal) | Ballooning, Mallory-Denk bodies | Ballooning, Steatosis | Variable, interface hepatitis | Minimal initially       |
| **Inflammation**        | Lymphocytic, portal aggregates | Neutrophilic        | Mixed                | Lymphocytic/Plasma cell | Lymphocytic (portal)    |
| **Fibrosis**            | Minimal/Absent              | Variable            | Variable             | Bridging/Cirrhosis | Variable, ductular reaction |
| **Acidophil Bodies**    | Present                     | Absent              | Absent               | Absent            | Absent                  |
| **Mallory-Denk Bodies** | Absent                      | Present             | Present (less common) | Absent            | Absent                  |
| **Steatosis**           | Minimal                     | Variable            | Present              | Minimal           | Absent                  |
| **Granulomas**          | Absent                      | Absent              | Absent               | Absent            | Cholestatic Granulomas |
| **Bile Duct Injury**    | Minimal                     | Absent              | Absent               | Minimal           | Present                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute viral hepatitis (like HAV) is characterized histologically by diffuse hepatocellular necrosis (often midzonal/periportal), a prominent lymphocytic infiltrate (especially in portal tracts forming lymphoid aggregates), apoptotic bodies (acidophil bodies), and cholestasis. Differentiating it from other liver diseases relies on recognizing these specific features and excluding characteristic findings of alcoholic hepatitis (Mallory-Denk bodies, neutrophilic infiltrate), NASH (steatosis, ballooning), chronic hepatitis (bridging fibrosis, interface hepatitis), and cholestatic diseases (granulomas, bile duct injury).

<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Liver Pathology: Focus 02-50 (Hepatitis Viruses)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the molecular mechanisms underlying liver injury in viral hepatitis, specifically focusing on the role of viral proteins in disrupting cellular processes and leading to hepatocellular damage (肝细胞损伤). It requires differentiating between various viral mechanisms and their associated histological features.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male intravenous drug user presents to the emergency department with a 3-week history of fatigue, jaundice, dark urine, and pale stools. He denies recent travel or exposure to sick individuals. His past medical history is significant for hypertension, managed with lisinopril. Physical examination reveals scleral icterus, mild hepatomegaly, and no splenomegaly or ascites. Laboratory tests show elevated AST (1200 U/L, normal <40 U/L), ALT (1500 U/L, normal <40 U/L), bilirubin (total 8 mg/dL, normal <1.2 mg/dL), and alkaline phosphatase (150 U/L, normal <120 U/L). Hepatitis A IgM, Hepatitis B surface antigen, and Hepatitis C antibody are all negative. However, Hepatitis E IgM is positive. A liver biopsy performed one week later shows diffuse hepatocellular necrosis, predominantly in the midzonal (zone 2) region, with minimal inflammation and no significant fibrosis. Scattered Councilman bodies (apoptotic hepatocytes) are present. Immunohistochemical staining reveals positive staining for Hepatitis E antigen within the hepatocytes.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed hepatocellular injury in this patient?

(A) Formation of immune complexes leading to complement-mediated lysis of hepatocytes.
(B) Inhibition of host cell protein synthesis by viral RNA polymerase.
(C) Induction of apoptosis via direct viral protein interference with mitochondrial function.
(D) Formation of viral inclusion bodies that disrupt cellular architecture and trigger inflammation.
(E) Induction of hepatocellular carcinoma through integration of viral DNA into the host genome.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male IV drug user (risk factor for bloodborne viruses, but tests are negative for HBV/HCV).
- **Symptoms:** Fatigue, jaundice, dark urine, pale stools (classic signs of acute hepatitis).
- **Physical Exam:** Scleral icterus, mild hepatomegaly (consistent with liver inflammation/damage).
- **Labs:** Markedly elevated AST/ALT (hepatocellular injury), elevated bilirubin (impaired excretion), normal/mildly elevated ALP (suggests primarily hepatocellular pattern, not cholestatic). Negative for HAV, HBV, HCV. Positive for HEV IgM (confirms acute Hepatitis E infection).
- **Liver Biopsy:** Diffuse hepatocellular necrosis, midzonal (zone 2) predominance, minimal inflammation, Councilman bodies (apoptotic bodies), positive HEV antigen staining.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism of hepatocellular injury.

The key clues are the diagnosis of acute Hepatitis E infection confirmed by positive IgM and the specific histological findings: diffuse midzonal necrosis with minimal inflammation and Councilman bodies. Hepatitis E virus (HEV) primarily causes hepatocellular injury through direct cytopathic effects, particularly apoptosis, rather than significant immune-mediated inflammation (unlike HAV or HBV). The midzonal necrosis pattern is characteristic of HEV. Councilman bodies are apoptotic hepatocytes.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Hepatitis E Virus (HEV):** A positive-sense, single-stranded RNA virus.
- **Pathogenesis:** HEV replicates primarily in hepatocytes. Unlike Hepatitis A and B viruses, HEV infection typically elicits a relatively weak immune response, resulting in less severe inflammation and less fibrosis.
- **Mechanism of Hepatocellular Injury:** The primary mechanism of liver damage in HEV infection is believed to be direct cytopathic effect of the virus on hepatocytes, leading to apoptosis. HEV proteins, particularly the ORF2 protein, are thought to interfere with mitochondrial function and cellular signaling pathways involved in apoptosis regulation, leading to programmed cell death. This direct viral effect explains the characteristic histological finding of hepatocellular necrosis with minimal inflammation. The midzonal (zone 2) necrosis is also a feature often associated with HEV.
- **Councilman Bodies:** These are eosinophilic, shrunken bodies representing hepatocytes undergoing apoptosis. Their presence supports the apoptotic mechanism of injury.

Now let's evaluate the options based on this understanding:

- **(A) Formation of immune complexes leading to complement-mediated lysis of hepatocytes:** This mechanism is more characteristic of Hepatitis B virus infection, where circulating immune complexes deposit in the liver and activate complement, leading to inflammation and damage. HEV typically causes less immune complex formation and less severe inflammation.
- **(B) Inhibition of host cell protein synthesis by viral RNA polymerase:** While viral replication does consume host resources, the primary mechanism of HEV-induced liver injury is not thought to be the direct inhibition of host protein synthesis by the viral polymerase. This is not the main cytopathic mechanism.
- **(C) Induction of apoptosis via direct viral protein interference with mitochondrial function:** This aligns well with the known pathogenesis of HEV. HEV proteins, especially ORF2, are implicated in inducing apoptosis in infected hepatocytes, leading to the observed necrosis and Councilman bodies with minimal inflammation. This fits the clinical and histological picture.
- **(D) Formation of viral inclusion bodies that disrupt cellular architecture and trigger inflammation:** While some viruses form inclusion bodies (e.g., CMV, HSV), HEV does not typically form prominent inclusion bodies that are a major cause of cellular disruption or inflammation. The damage is more related to direct cytopathic effects and apoptosis.
- **(E) Induction of hepatocellular carcinoma through integration of viral DNA into the host genome:** This mechanism is characteristic of Hepatitis B virus (HBV), which is a DNA virus that can integrate into the host genome and promote malignant transformation. HEV is an RNA virus and does not integrate into the host genome; it is not typically associated with hepatocellular carcinoma.

Therefore, the most likely mechanism responsible for the hepatocellular injury in this patient with acute Hepatitis E is the induction of apoptosis via direct viral protein interference with cellular processes, including mitochondrial function.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Formation of immune complexes leading to complement-mediated lysis of hepatocytes:** Incorrect. This is the primary mechanism in Hepatitis B, characterized by significant inflammation, deposition of HBsAg and anti-HBs complexes, and complement activation. HEV causes minimal inflammation.
- **(B) Inhibition of host cell protein synthesis by viral RNA polymerase:** Incorrect. While viral replication occurs, the main cytopathic effect of HEV is apoptosis, not generalized inhibition of host protein synthesis. This is not the primary mechanism of liver damage.
- **(D) Formation of viral inclusion bodies that disrupt cellular architecture and trigger inflammation:** Incorrect. HEV does not typically form prominent inclusion bodies like CMV or HSV. The damage is primarily due to direct cytopathic effects and apoptosis, not disruption by large inclusions.
- **(E) Induction of hepatocellular carcinoma through integration of viral DNA into the host genome:** Incorrect. This is characteristic of HBV (a DNA virus). HEV is an RNA virus and does not integrate into the host genome. Chronic HEV infection is not strongly linked to HCC, unlike chronic HBV or HCV.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Hepatitis A (HAV) | Hepatitis B (HBV) | Hepatitis C (HCV) | Hepatitis E (HEV) |
|-------------------------|-------------------|-------------------|-------------------|-------------------|
| Virus Type              | RNA               | DNA               | RNA               | RNA               |
| Genome Integration      | No                | Yes (sometimes)   | No                | No                |
| Primary Pathogenesis    | Immune-mediated   | Immune-mediated   | Immune-mediated   | Direct cytopathic/Apoptosis |
| Inflammation Level      | High              | High              | Moderate/High     | Low/Minimal       |
| Typical Histology       | Portal inflammation | Ground-glass hepatocytes, inflammation | Lymphoid aggregates, inflammation | Midzonal necrosis, apoptosis, minimal inflammation |
| Inclusion Bodies        | No                | No                | No                | No                |
| Immune Complexes        | Yes               | Yes               | Yes               | Minimal           |
| HCC Association         | No                | Yes               | Yes               | Low               |
| Key Mechanism of Injury | Immune response   | Immune response   | Immune response   | Apoptosis         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hepatitis E virus (HEV) causes acute hepatitis primarily through direct cytopathic effects on hepatocytes, leading to apoptosis and characteristic midzonal necrosis with minimal inflammation. This contrasts with HAV, HBV, and HCV, where immune-mediated mechanisms play a more significant role, resulting in more pronounced inflammation and different histological patterns. Remember HEV is an RNA virus, does not integrate, and is not strongly associated with HCC. Differentiate HEV pathogenesis from HBV (immune complexes, DNA integration, HCC) and HAV (immune-mediated inflammation).
